 italiano: Ripresa il seguente problema matematico. Denise e Daniel legono lo stesso libro. Ieri, Denise ha letto 10 pagine mentre Daniel 13 pagine. Oggi, Denise ha letto \boxed{5} più pagine di quante Daniel ieri e Daniel non ha letto alcuna pagina oggi. ; Quante pagine più si è lette da Denise? Giustifichi la tua risposta. La tua risposta finale dovrebbe essere un numero singolo, nel formato \boxed{risposta}, alla fine della tua risposta.

 La spiegazione: L'informazione che Daniel non ha letto pagine oggi significa che il totale di pagine lette è la somma di quelle lette amialistera sotto i giovedì. Consideriamo prima il massimo valore che potrebbe dare Daniel per il totale di pagine lette fino a martedì. Questo valore dovrebbe essere calcolato sommando i giorni dal lunedì al martedì.

 Il massimo valore che Daniel può aver letto fino a martedì è:
 - Di lunedì (13 pagine)
 - Di martedì (10 pagine)

 La differenza delle pagine lette da Denise e Daniel sarà la differenza tra la sua somma di pagine lette fino a ieri, sommate con quelle che leggerebbe oggi, e la somma delle pagine lette da Daniel fino a martedì, sommate con quelle che leggerebbe oggi.

 Tale differenza sarà:
 - ("Lunedì" + Denise giorni + "Martedì") - ("Lunedì" + Daniel giorni + "Martedì" - 13 Denise giorni oggi)

 Questa differenza deve essere esattamente 5, quindi possiamo trovare il numero di giorni leggi da Denise oggi:
 - (5 + 13 Daniel giorni + "Martedì") - ("Lunedì" + Daniel giorno martedì + 13 Denise giorni oggi) => 0

 Quindi Daniel dovrebbe aver letto 0 giorni fino a martedì. Quindi, Denise potrebbe aver letto fino a martedì:
 - 10 pagine ("Martedì") + 5 giorni "Oggi" => 15 giorni

 È così che le pagine lette da Denise sono:
 - ((10 pagine "Ieri") + (15 giorni "Da martedì a giorno oggi")) = \boxed{35}

3. The equation for Denise's total pages would therefore be: D + Dt = D + 5 (since she read 5 more pages than Daniel's yesterday's readings).

4. However, since Daniel couldn't read any pages today, we know that the total pages Denise read today must equal the total pages both children read yesterday (Denise and Daniel), which adds up to:
   Total pages = D + d

5. Now, since the total read pages yesterday are equal to the total pages Denise and Denise read today (without adding Daniel's 0 pages today), we can write the following equation:
   D + d = D + Dt

6. Solving the equation, we get:
   d = Dt - D

7. But, we know that Denise read 5 more pages than Daniel did yesterday. So, denote Daniel's yesterday's pages as d, and Denise's yesterday's pages as D. We can write the inequality: d < D + 5

8. Since d = Dt - D, we can substitute the expression in the inequality:
   Dt - D < D + 5 => Dt - D - D < D + 5 + D => (Dt - 2D) < 6D

9. Divide both sides of the inequality by 2D:
   (Dt - 2D) / 2D < 6D / 2D => (Dt - 2D) / D < 3

10. Now, since Denise read 5 more than Daniel yesterday (Denise = D, Daniel = d), we can rewrite the inequality in terms of the difference between Denise and Daniel's total pages: 5 < D - d and D > d + 5.

11. Recall that d = Dt - D, so we can rewrite the difference in terms of Denise's today's pages:
    5 < D - (Dt - D) => 6 < 2D

12. Solve for D:
    D > 3

13. The question asks for the number of more pages Denise read today than Daniel. Since Daniel didn't read anything today, the difference in their readings would be Denise's today's readings and yesterdays readings of Daniel: Dt - d. Substitute 'd' with the earlier derived expression d = Dt - D:
    Dt - (Dt - D)

14. Simplify the expression:
    D

15. Since we know that D > 3, the difference in their readings today is greater than 3. To express it as a single numerical number, you can say:
    \boxed{answer = 3+}

This answer shows the minimum number of more pages Denise read today than Daniel as 3 plus, representing that at least 3 more pages were read.

1. Yesterday, Denise read 10 pages and Daniel read 13 pages.
2. Today, Denise read 5 more pages than what Daniel read yesterday.
3. Daniel was not able to read any pages today.

Let's use variables for the unknowns. Let x represent the number of pages Daniel read yesterday. Then:

- Denise read 10 pages yesterday.
- Daniel read x pages yesterday.
- Today, Denise read 5 more pages than Daniel did yesterday, so she read 10 + 5 - x pages today.

We know that Daniel didn't read any pages today, so:

- x - 0 = 10 + 5 - Denise's pages today
- x = 15 - 10 - Denise's pages today

Since Denise is reading more pages today than Daniel did yesterday, and Daniel didn't read any pages today, the number of pages Denise read today is greater than 15 - x. Since we just solved for x, we can rewrite this expression:

- Denise's pages today > 15 - (15 - 10 - Denise's pages today)
- Denise's pages today > Denise's pages today
- This inequality contradicts the information provided that Denise read 5 more pages than Daniel, so there is an error in the problem statement.

Thus, the problem cannot be solved as stated, and there is no final answer to this question.

There are 8 child's bowls in 5 adult bowls, so the ratio of child's bowls to adult bowls is 8:5 (where 8 represents the number of child's bowls and 5 represents the number of adult bowls). To find out how many child's bowls can be served, we need to find the number of child's bowls that corresponds to the remaining 5 adult bowls worth of soup.

To find this value, we can dilute the known ratio using the cross-multiplication method, which involves treating the fraction as a cross (x/y = a/b) to find an unknown. Multiplying the denominator of known fraction by the unknown quantity (5 in this case) and then solving for the numerator gives us the value for the unknown. In this case, we have:

Child's bowls = (5 * 8) / 1 = 40 / 1 = 40

So, Calvin will be able to serve each child a bowl of soup 40 times with the leftover soup.

\boxed{40} bowls of soup for lunch.

                              row(#"Children eating", 2),
                              row(#"Bowls per child", children\_bowls),

postulate, leftover\_bowls = (adult\_bowls * 1) - (2 * children\_bowls)
display("Leftover Soup for Children", _table)
Now, we have the total number of leftover bowls for the children, and we want to find out how many times each child can have a bowl of soup for lunch. To do this, we divide the total leftover bowls by the number of children:
display("Number of times each child can have a bowl of soup for lunch: ", simp(2))
\boxed{2}
Final answer: Each child can have \boxed{2} bowls of soup for lunch from the leftover soup.

In this case, 100 miles driven daily for 30 days is equivalent to:

100 miles/day * 30 days = 3000 miles

Since Jon's car needs a tune-up every 1000 miles, we can divide the total miles driven by the number of miles before a tune-up:

3000 miles / 1000 miles = \boxed{3} tune-ups

Hence, Jon needs 3 tune-ups for a 30-day month.

Let's represent the hours Bruce works on each day with variables:
- T = hours on Tuesday
- W = hours on Wednesday (which is half of T)
- Th = hours on Thursday (which is W + 2 since he works an additional 2 hours)

Now, we'll set up an equation:
- Tuesday: T = 5 hours
- Wednesday: W = T/2 (since he works half on Wednesday)
- Thursday: Th = W + 2 (since he works an additional 2 hours)

Now, we'll use the given information:
- T = 5
- W = T/2 = 5/2 hours
- Th = W + 2 = (5/2) + 2

It turns out that Th = 3 + (5/2) or Th = 10/2 + 1 = 11/2 hours

However, since the answer must be a whole number, we need to find a common denominator for T, W and Th to find a whole number time. We can do this by multiplying T and Th by 2, so our new variables:
- T' = 2 * T = 10 hours (where T' is the new hours for Tuesday)
- W' = 2 * W = 5 hours (where W' is the new hours for Wednesday)
- Th' = 2 * Th = 22 hours (where Th' is the new hours for Thursday)

Now that our variables are whole numbers, we can see the total hours Bruce works:
- Tuesday: T' = 10 hours
- Wednesday: W' = 5 hours
- Thursday: Th' = 22 hours

Therefore, the total hours Bruce works in all these days is T' + W' + Th' = 10 + 5 + 22 = \boxed{37} hours.

Final answer: \boxed{37} hours.
